Rényi-Induced Information Geometry and Hartigan’s Prior Family Journal: Differential Geometry and Its Applications
Rebecca Maria Kuntz, Heinrich von Campe, Björn Malte Schäfer

Abstract
We derive the information geometry induced by the statistical Rényi divergence, namely its metric tensor, its dual parametrized connections, as well as its dual Laplacians. Based on these results, we demonstrate that the Rényi-geometry, though closely related, differs in structure from Amari’s well-known α \alpha -geometry. Subsequently, we derive the canonical uniform prior distributions for a statistical manifold endowed with a Rényi-geometry, namely the dual Rényi-covolumes. We find that the Rényi-priors can be made to coincide with Takeuchi and Amari’s α \alpha -priors by a reparameterization, which is itself of particular significance in statistics. Herewith, we demonstrate that Hartigan’s parametrized ( α H \alpha_{H} ) family of priors is precisely the parametrized ( ρ \rho ) family of Rényi-priors ( α H = ρ ) (\alpha_{H}=\rho) .



1
Introduction

The field of information geometry studies the innate geometry of statistics, opening a new, intuitive way to reason about the geometric invariants of statistical model families. In contrast to the geometries induced by Amari’s
\alpha
-divergences
[
1
]
, the geometry induced by the statistical Rényi divergence, whose associated entropy fulfills the generalized Shannon-Khinchin axioms
[
2
,
3
]
, remains largely unstudied. Motivated by this gap, this work constructs the Rényi-geometry, key quantities of which are contextualized with statistical estimators so as to discern their significance
for statistical inference. Evaluation of the Rényi-geometry against the backdrop of Amari’s
\alpha
-geometry
[
1
]
shows their inherent difference in structure. Dual affine volume forms are derived for the Rényi-geometry, leading to the novel Rényi-priors, which are shown to precisely coincide with Hartigan’s parametrized prior family. This result offers a new geometric reasoning for a result by Takeuchi and Amari
[
4
]
.

Previous works on geometries related to the Rényi divergence include de Souza, Vigelis and Cavalcante
[
5
]
, who use the Rényi divergence to study a generalization of the
\alpha
-geometry. Furthermore, van Erven and Harremoës
[
6
]
give a review of the Rényi divergence and its properties. Studies addressing divergence-induced canonical prior choices in information geometry include those by Takeuchi and Amari
[
4
]
and Jiang, Tavakoli and Zhao
[
7
]
.

The functional idea of information geometry begins with a sample (data) space
\mathscr{Y}
endowed with a suitable integration
measure
\differential\mu
. Furthermore, let
p
denote a probability
distribution (statistical model),

p:\Theta\to\mathcal{P}(\mathscr{Y}).

(1)

on
\mathscr{Y}
. Here,
\Theta\subseteq\mathds{R}^{n}
is referred to as the parameter space.
\mathcal{P}(\mathscr{Y})
denotes a certain set of such models. Subsequently, an information
geometry can be constructed on the topological space constituted by such a model
family
\mathcal{P}
.

The most prominent examples of such families are the
exponential family
[
1
]

\mathcal{P}_{e}=\Big\{p_{\theta}(y)=\exp\left(\theta^{i}y_{i}+k(y)-\psi(\theta)\right)\Big\}_{\theta\in\Theta}\,,

(2)

with
k:\mathscr{Y}\rightarrow\mathds{R}
a function of the data
y
,
\psi:\Theta\rightarrow\mathds{R}
a normalization function,
as well as the mixture family
[
1
,
8
]

\mathcal{P}_{m}=\Big\{p_{\eta}(y)=\eta^{i}F_{i}(y)+C(y)\Big\}_{\eta\in\Theta}\,,

(3)

with
F_{i}:\mathscr{Y}\to\mathds{R}
some linearly independent functions such that
\smallint_{\mathscr{Y}}F_{i}(y)=0
and
C:\mathscr{Y}\to\mathds{R}
a function s.t.

\smallint_{\mathscr{Y}}C(y)=1
.

The key insight of information geometry is that families of probability
distributions such as the above may be described as so-called statistical
manifolds
[
1
,
8
]
. In this picture, the parameters
locally provide a coordinate chart
[
9
]
. This is a generalization of the more classical picture
where model parameters live on
\mathds{R}^{n}
. An illustration
of this interpretation is that the reparametrization of a model
p
is now
interpreted as a mere change of coordinates on the corresponding statistical
manifold
\mathcal{M}

[
8
]
.

The aim of information geometry is to define
geometric structures on these manifolds in a way that illuminates the inherent
geometric properties of the statistical models. Rao’s highly innovative work on the Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence
[
10
]
uncovered the key role played by the the Fisher information metric
[
11
]
in information geometry,

\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{F}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}g^{{\kern-7.46219pt{\text{F}}\kern 4.15245pt\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-14.49915pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.15245pt{i}{j}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{F}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}g^{{\kern-7.46219pt{\text{F}}\kern 4.15245pt\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-14.49915pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.15245pt{i}{j}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{F}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}g^{{\kern-4.90878pt{\text{F}}\kern 2.51294pt\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-9.93518pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.51294pt{i}{j}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{F}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}g^{{\kern-4.1908pt{\text{F}}\kern 1.79495pt\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-9.2172pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.79495pt{i}{j}}}}(\theta):=\int\differential{y}p\,\partial_{i}\log p\,\partial_{j}\log p\,,

(4)

since it promotes the statistical manifold to a metric space and is invariant under sufficient statistics
[
1
]
.

The Kullback-Leibler divergence in conjunction with the Fisher information is widely used in machine learning and
statistical inference for methods such as Natural Gradient Descent
[
12
]

and Riemann Manifold
Hamilton Monte Carlo
[
13
]
.

Given this metric tensor, Efron and Dawid

[
14
]
found that the most natural construction
from an information geometric perspective are
two
connections (the
so-called
e
and
m
connections) which are
dually
metric compatible. It was Amari who recognized that information geometry is thus dual in essence
[
1
]
. Building on
this, canonical dual prior distributions can be derived from volume forms which
are
parallel
to (preserved under) such dual connections.

The most famous example of such geometric priors is Jeffreys prior,
which follows from the volume form parallel to the Levi-Civita connection with
the Fisher metric
[
15
]
.
Figure 1
schematically depicts the construction of different layers of geometrical structure on a topological space.

Figure 1:
Geometric structures are succesively built on a topological space.

A natural way to construct the aforementioned geometry on a statistical manifold
is from statistical divergences. Loosely speaking, a statistical divergence or relative entropy is a
macroscopic dissimilarity measure between probability distributions
[
8
,
16
]
. A canonical choice for such a divergence is the KL divergence, whose associated entropy, the Shannon entropy, is axiomatically singled out by the so-called Shannon-Khinchin axioms
[
2
]
. The generalized Shannon-Khinchin axioms lead to the Rényi entropy as a generalization of the Shannon entropy, making the Rényi divergence a suitable choice for inducing an information geometry
[
3
]
.

Eguchi
[
17
]
demonstrates that any such divergence permits the definition of a
metric and a pair of dual connections. The overall geometric structure of the manifold thus not only depends on
the statistical model at hand but also the statistical divergence that induces different geometric objects.

This paper is organized as follows: Section
2
discusses several exisiting divergence-induced geometries before presenting the Rényi divergence’s induced geometry. Section
3
derives the dual
Laplace-Beltrami operators for the Rényi-geometry. Subsequently,
Section

4
reviews various existing canonical
priors of information geometry before deriving the Rényi-priors from volume forms
parallel to the dual Rényi-connections which are shown to coincide with Hartigan’s prior family.
Finally, Section
5
summarizes our results and
gives a synopsis of possible next steps.

2
Rényi Metric and Connections

2.1
Preliminaries I: (Dual) Connections on (Statistical) Manifolds

Given a manifold
\mathcal{M}
, a connection
\nabla:\Gamma(T\mathcal{M})\times\Gamma(T\mathcal{M})\to\Gamma(T\mathcal{M})

is a covariant generalization of the directional derivative. Here,
\Gamma(T\mathcal{M})
is the associated tangent bundle. The connection is bilinear and fulfills

[
18
]

\nabla_{fV}Y=f\nabla_{V}Y\,,\quad\nabla_{V}(fY)=f\nabla_{V}Y+V(f)Y\,,

(5)

for any vector fields
V,Y\in\Gamma(T\mathcal{M})
and any smooth function

f\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
. It may be fully characterized
by its
connection coefficients

(\nabla_{\partial_{i}}(\partial_{j}))^{k}=-(\nabla_{\partial_{i}}\differential{x}^{k})_{j}=\mathchoice{\Gamma^{{{k}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.98645pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.84746pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{ij}}}}{\Gamma^{{{k}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.98645pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.84746pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{ij}}}}{\Gamma^{{{k}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.98645pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.84746pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{ij}}}}{\Gamma^{{{k}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.98645pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.84746pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{ij}}}}
.

If the manifold
\mathcal{M}
is equipped with a metric
g
, we may furthermore define the
notion of
metric compatibility
of an affine connection
\nabla

w. r. t. the metric
g
by

[
18
]

Zg(X,Y)=g(\mathchoice{\nabla^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.93889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.93889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.6954pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.63957pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Z}}}}{\nabla^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.93889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.93889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.6954pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.63957pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Z}}}}{\nabla^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.93889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.93889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.6954pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.63957pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Z}}}}{\nabla^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.93889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.93889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.6954pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.63957pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Z}}}}X,Y)+g(X,\mathchoice{\nabla^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.93889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.93889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.6954pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.63957pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Z}}}}{\nabla^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.93889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.93889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.6954pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.63957pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Z}}}}{\nabla^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.93889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.93889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.6954pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.63957pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Z}}}}{\nabla^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.93889pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.93889pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.6954pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.63957pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Z}}}}Y)\,,

(6)

for arbitrary vector fields
X,Y,Z\in\Gamma(T\mathcal{M})
. This
equation directly implies that the the inner product of two vectors
g(X,Y)
that are
parallel transported along a curve
\gamma
by means of a connection
\nabla
(i. e.
\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}X=0
) will remain
constant if the connection is metric compatible.

One commonly uses the Levi-Civita connection

\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-14.75882pt{\text{LC}}\kern 8.04631pt}}_{{\kern-29.28188pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.04631pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-14.75882pt{\text{LC}}\kern 8.04631pt}}_{{\kern-29.28188pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.04631pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-10.27547pt{\text{LC}}\kern 5.44908pt}}_{{\kern-20.64906pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.44908pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-9.1088pt{\text{LC}}\kern 4.28241pt}}_{{\kern-19.48239pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.28241pt}}}

which is the unique torsion-free metric-compatible connection

[
18
]
. Information geometry on the other hand is built on the notion of
dual metric
compatibility
of
two
connections
\nabla,\nabla^{*}
w. r. t. a
metric
g
, defined by

[
8
]

Zg(X,Y)=g(\nabla_{Z}X,Y)+g(X,\nabla^{*}_{Z}Y)\,.

(7)

In general, neither
\nabla
nor
\nabla^{*}
are metric compatible by
themselves. In analogy to the description above,
Equation 7
indicates that the inner
product of two vectors
g(X,Y)
remains constant along a curve
\gamma
if
one of the vectors is parallel transported by means of
\nabla
and the other
with
\nabla^{*}

[
8
]
. One may easily see that the arithmetic mean of the
two is indeed the Levi-Civita connection,

\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-14.75882pt{\text{LC}}\kern 8.04631pt}}_{{\kern-29.28188pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.04631pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-14.75882pt{\text{LC}}\kern 8.04631pt}}_{{\kern-29.28188pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.04631pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-10.27547pt{\text{LC}}\kern 5.44908pt}}_{{\kern-20.64906pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.44908pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-9.1088pt{\text{LC}}\kern 4.28241pt}}_{{\kern-19.48239pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.28241pt}}}=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla+\nabla^{*})
.
The difference between different connection coefficients is always a
tensor
[
19
]
.
Connections allow to study the notion of curvature of
manifolds
[
18
]
. Note that a manifold is called flat (has zero curvature) if there exists a coordinate choice for which the connection coefficients vanish globally.

2.2
Preliminaries II: Divergence-Induced Information Geometries

Given a statistical manifold
\mathcal{M}
and a statistical divergence

D:\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}\to\mathds{R}_{+}\cup\{0\}
with

D[\theta:\theta^{\prime}]=0\leftrightarrow\theta=\theta^{\prime}\,,\quad\partial_{i}D[\theta:\theta^{\prime}]|_{\theta=\theta^{\prime}}=0=\partial_{j^{\prime}}D[\theta:\theta^{\prime}]|_{\theta=\theta^{\prime}}\,,

(8)

-\partial_{i}\partial_{j^{\prime}}D[\theta:\theta^{\prime}]|_{\theta=\theta^{\prime}}\quad\text{is positive definite}\,,

(9)

Eguchi
[
17
]
demonstrated that one may construct a
divergence-induced information geometry on
\mathcal{M}
with the following quantities:

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{D}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.75377pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.75377pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.1929pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.99492pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}g^{{\kern-8.45647pt{D}\kern 4.15245pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.98645pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.84746pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-17.68085pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.75377pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.75377pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.1929pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.99492pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.15245pt{ij}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{D}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.75377pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.75377pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.1929pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.99492pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}g^{{\kern-8.45647pt{D}\kern 4.15245pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.98645pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.84746pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-17.68085pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.75377pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.75377pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.1929pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.99492pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.15245pt{ij}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{D}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.75377pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.75377pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.1929pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.99492pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}g^{{\kern-5.61897pt{D}\kern 2.51294pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.98645pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.84746pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-12.2078pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.75377pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.75377pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.1929pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.99492pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.51294pt{ij}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{D}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.75377pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.75377pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.1929pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.99492pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}g^{{\kern-4.90099pt{D}\kern 1.79495pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.98645pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.84746pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-11.4898pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.75377pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.75377pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.1929pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.99492pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.79495pt{ij}}}}=-\partial_{i}\partial_{j^{\prime}}D[\theta:\theta^{\prime}]\big|_{\theta=\theta^{\prime}}\,,\quad

\displaystyle\text{metric tensor},

(10)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{D}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.75377pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.75377pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.1929pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.99492pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Gamma^{{\kern-9.24847pt{D}\kern \displaystyle\text{dual connection coefficient of first kind}.

(12)

One may easily check that the behaviour of these quantities under coordinate
transformations on
\mathcal{M}

[
18
]

is correct.
Please note that
these are the connection coefficients of the first kind which are related to the
aforementioned connection coefficients of the second kind by

\Gamma^{k}_{ij}=g^{k\ell}\Gamma_{ij\ell}

[
18
]
.

]
,

(18)

The metric may be identified as the Fisher metric. It is a well fact in
information geometry
[
8
]

and uniquely characterized by its invariance under
sufficient statistics, that is, transformations of the data space that preserve the
data’s information content about the parameters
[
1
,
8
]

(24)

Firstly, note that by taking the limit of
\alpha\to-1
, we recover the Kullback-Leibler case:

\lim_{\alpha\to-1}D_{\alpha}=D_{\text{KL}}\,,\quad\lim_{\alpha\to-1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\alpha^{*})}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.39879pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.39879pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.19563pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.21118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-17.35306pt{(\alpha^{*})}\kern 8.04631pt}}_{{\kern-38.26343pt\mathchoice{\makebox[14.39879pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.39879pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.19563pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.21118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.5
Rényi’s
\rho
-Geometry

While the
\alpha
-geometry has been well studied in the past, the
geometry induced by the Rényi divergence
1
1

1

Note: To avoid confusion with

with
\rho

R
ényi) instead of

.

[
23
,
6
]
,

(26)

with

Note that for the coordinate-free expressions on the right in

and

the connection coefficients of the second kind were
used (
\nabla_{i}X^{j}:=\partial_{i}X^{j}+\mathchoice{\Gamma^{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.24945pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.24945pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.39331pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13808pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{ik}}}}{\Gamma^{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.24945pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.24945pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.39331pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13808pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{ik}}}}{\Gamma^{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.24945pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.24945pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.39331pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13808pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{ik}}}}{\Gamma^{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.24945pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.24945pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.39331pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13808pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{ik}}}}X^{k}
). To raise indices, the inverse of the Rényi-metric

must be used, contributing an additional factor

as compared

(32)

This asymptotic behavior differs from the Amari geometry (
\alpha\to-1
but

\rho\to+1
), due to the historic definitions of the divergences.

While the two geometries are
similar in the regard that they both admit a tensorial difference between the
connection
\nabla
and its dual
\nabla^{*}
, which is given by a scaled version
of the Amari-Chentsov tensor
C
, they differ in two significant ways: On the

fulfill dual metric compatibility with respect to
{}^{(\rho)}g
since they are divergence-induced.)
Note
that the conformal factor does not affect the invariance under sufficient
statistics, since the Rényi metric is still proportional to the Fisher metric.

In fact, one may try to make the two geometries match by introducing a
coordinate transformation that alters the Rényi geometry,

(34)

do not take the same form as in the
\alpha
geometry. Thus, while they share
some properties, the two geometries are genuinely different.

Lastly, consider the so-called Bhattacharyya distance
[
24
]
,

D_{\text{B}}[\theta:\theta^{\prime}]:=D_{(\rho=\frac{1}{2})}[\theta:\theta^{\prime}]=-2\log\int\differential\mu(y)\,\sqrt{pp^{\prime}}\,,

(35)

a symmetric statistical divergence which emerges from Rényi (
Equation 26
) for the choice of
\rho=\frac{1}{2}
. Due to this divergence’s symmetry,
D_{\text{B}}[\theta:\theta^{\prime}]=D_{\text{B}}[\theta^{\prime}:\theta]
, its induced information geometry is
non-dual
, i.e.

. Clearly, the Bhattacharyya-geometry is
particular, in that its symmetry erases the usual duality of information geometry.

3
Dual Rényi-Laplace-Beltrami Operators

3.1
Preliminaries: (Generalized) Vector Calculus in Information Geometry

Generalized vector calculus on statistical manifolds is significant from both the geometric as well as the statistical angle. Consider again a manifold
\mathcal{M}
, a metric tensor
g
, as well as a connection
\nabla
. In differential geometry, the divergence of a vector field is defined as

\text{div}:\Gamma(T\mathcal{M})\to\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{M}),\quad X\mapsto\text{tr}\,\nabla X\;(=\nabla_{\partial_{i}}X^{i})\,.

(38)

From a geometric standpoint, the divergence quantifies the rate at which the size of volume elements changes as they move along the flow of a vector field
[
25
]
. On a
statistical
manifold, such changes in volume correspond to changes in the enclosed probability mass. Commonly, the LC-connection’s divergence may be expressed as

\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\text{div}^{{\kern-17.59451pt{\text{LC}}\kern 10.882pt}}_{{\kern-32.11757pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 10.882pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\text{div}^{{\kern-17.59451pt{\text{LC}}\kern 10.882pt}}_{{\kern-32.11757pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 10.882pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\text{div}^{{\kern-11.49585pt{\text{LC}}\kern 6.66946pt}}_{{\kern-21.86945pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.66946pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\text{div}^{{\kern-9.59027pt{\text{LC}}\kern 4.76389pt}}_{{\kern-19.96387pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.76389pt}}}X=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\det\mathchoice{g}{g}{g}{g}}}\partial_{j}\left(\sqrt{\det\mathchoice{g}{g}{g}{g}}X^{j}\right)\,,

(39)

in terms of a metric tensor
g
. Besides, the gradient of a smooth function,

\text{grad}:\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{M})\to\Gamma(T\mathcal{M}),\quad h\mapsto\,(\differential h)^{\sharp}\;\left(=g^{ij}\partial_{j}h\right)\,,

(40)

(with
\sharp
the musical isomorphism) allows to define the generalized Laplacian or Laplace-Beltrami operator,

\Delta:\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{M})\to\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{M}),\quad h\mapsto\text{div}\,\text{grad}\,h=\partial_{i}(\mathchoice{g^{{{ik}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.24945pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.24945pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.39331pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13808pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{g^{{{ik}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.24945pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.24945pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.39331pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13808pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{g^{{{ik}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.24945pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.24945pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.39331pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13808pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{g^{{{ik}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.24945pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.24945pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.39331pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13808pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\,\partial_{k}h)+\mathchoice{\Gamma^{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.98645pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.84746pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{ij}}}}{\Gamma^{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.98645pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.84746pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{ij}}}}{\Gamma^{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.98645pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.84746pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{ij}}}}{\Gamma^{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.54285pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.98645pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.84746pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{ij}}}}\;\mathchoice{g^{{{i\ell}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.17654pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.17654pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72978pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.66414pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{g^{{{i\ell}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.17654pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.17654pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72978pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.66414pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{g^{{{i\ell}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.17654pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.17654pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72978pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.66414pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{g^{{{i\ell}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.17654pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.17654pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72978pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.66414pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\,\partial_{\ell}h\,.

(41)

Laplace-Beltrami operators are of great significance in statistics as they allow to evaluate the admissibility of statistical estimators, such as Bayes estimators, in the form of risk differences
[
26
,
27
,
28
,
29
]
. Consequently, it is our view that the parametrized Rényi-Laplacians derived in the following (in conjunction with the novel Rényi-priors, see Section
4
), may open alternative ways to judge the optimality of decision rules in statistics.

(45)

Reparametrizing with
\rho(\alpha)=\frac{1-\alpha}{2}
clarifies that the Rényi-divergences are equivalent to those of Amari’s
\alpha
-geometry given in
Equation 42
. The significance of this reparameterization will be the subject of a detailed discussion in Section
4.4
.

To find the dual RLB-operators, consider once again smooth function
h\in\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{M})

thus verifying Section

’s finding that the

- and Rényi-geometry are genuinely different.

Looking forward, we wish to employ this novel parametrized Laplacian
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\rho)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.34447pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.53177pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-13.02505pt{(\rho)}\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-26.9829pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.34447pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.53177pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\rho)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.34447pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.53177pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-13.02505pt{(\rho)}\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-26.9829pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.34447pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.53177pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\rho)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.34447pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.53177pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-8.72623pt{(\rho)}\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-18.6961pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.34447pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.53177pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\rho)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.34447pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.53177pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-7.55956pt{(\rho)}\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-17.52943pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.34447pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.53177pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.91667pt}}}
to evaluate the admissibility of statistical estimators
\delta
as well as predictive densities. As indicated by Hartigan
[
28
]
, Laplace-Beltrami-operators are commonly used to approximate
risk differences

R(\theta,\delta_{\pi})-R(\theta,\delta_{\text{MLE}})
of statistical estimators (with
\delta_{\pi}
the Bayes estimator and
\delta_{\text{MLE}}
the maximum likelihood estimator) as developed e.g. by Komaki
[
29
]
. Here,
\{\theta\}
indicates a coordinate frame on the statistical manifold, while
R(\theta,\delta):=\smallint\differential\mu(y)\,p_{\theta}(y)(\delta(y)-\theta)^{2}
is the so-called
risk
of the estimator in question (as defined in e.g. Brown
[
26
]
, Komaki
[
29
]
). Moreover, in later works, Komaki
[
31
,
32
]
uses the Laplacian
\Delta
(for the LC-connection and the Fisher information metric) to quantify the risk difference between so-called predictive densities in statistical inference.
In view of these promising applications, we plan to explore the effect that the
\rho
-scaling of the RLB-operators
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\rho)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.34447pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.53177pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-13.02505pt{(\rho)}\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-26.9829pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.34447pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.53177pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\rho)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.34447pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.53177pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-13.02505pt{(\rho)}\kern 6.56947pt}}_{{\kern-26.9829pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.34447pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.53177pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.56947pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\rho)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.34447pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.53177pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-8.72623pt{(\rho)}\kern 4.08334pt}}_{{\kern-18.6961pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.34447pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.53177pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.08334pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\rho)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.34447pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.53177pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Delta^{{\kern-7.55956pt{(\rho)}\kern 2.91667pt}}_{{\kern-17.52943pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.34447pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.53177pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.91667pt}}}

from Section
4.4
) has in deciding which estimator or predictive density is to be preferred over (i.e. has lower risk than) another.

4
Rényi Volume Forms, Rényi-Priors

4.1
Preliminaries I: Covolumes as Priors

Information geometry brought the geometric structure of statistical models to light, showing that parameter space is oftentimes non-Euclidean in nature. This important recognition comes with new challenges:
\differential^{n}\theta
is no longer the correct integration measure on these curved manifolds
[
1
]
.

To see why this is of key importance for statistics, consider e.g. the selection of a uniform prior in a Bayesian inference problem. In Bayesian inference, a prior belief (a probability distribution
\pi(\theta)
) shapes e.g. the evidence
p(y)=\smallint\differential^{n}\theta\;\pi(\theta)\;p_{\theta}(y)
, statistical estimators etc.
[
1
]
. In cases where there is no a priori information about the parameters of interest, the most uninformative prior must be selected to ensure fairness. However, identifying the form of the uninformative (also called
“uniform”
in the literature) prior distribution over a non-Euclidean (possibly non-flat) statistical manifold is a highly non-trivial task. A prior which does
not
correctly account for the statistical manifold’s geometry contaminates the inference result, introducing unjustified bias in the form of e.g. artifacts of certain coordinate choices.

In Euclidean space, integration over an (improper) uniform prior is done by
\smallint\differential^{n}\theta\;\pi(\theta)=\smallint\differential^{n}\theta\cdot 1
. However, for a curved parameter space, the measure
\differential^{n}\theta
does not assign equal probability mass to regions of equal volume, spoiling uniformity on the non-Euclidean statistical manifold
[
33
]
. This issue must be fixed by a suitable prior choice, which acts as a weighting, a
covolume
, that correctly restores the invariance of the integration measure, i.e.
\smallint\differential^{n}\theta\;\pi(\theta):=\smallint\omega
. Information geometry sometimes refers to such
“covolume priors”
as
canonical
, since they incorporate the geometric structure of the statistical models at hand
[
34
]
. This Section derives the Rényi-geometry’s priors, so as to correctly quantify the size of uncertainty regions for inference problems where the Rényi divergence is a favorable choice.

4.2
Preliminaries II: Volume Forms in Differential Geometry

As before, consider an
n
-dimensional oriented Riemannian manifold
(\mathcal{M},g)

[
25
]
. A
volume form
is a nowhere-vanishing
n
-form, which is given by

\omega=\text{cov}\;\differential x^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\differential x^{n}\;\in\Omega^{n}(\mathcal{M})=\Gamma(\Lambda^{n}\,T^{*}\mathcal{M})\,,

(51)

with the local induced coframe
\{\differential x^{i}\}
and
T^{*}\mathcal{M}
the cotangent bundle
[
35
,
36
]
. The coefficient function
\text{cov}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
is called the
covolume
.

This work focuses on
affine
volume forms, i.e. volume forms which are parallel with respect to a certain affine connection
\nabla

[
4
]
,

\nabla\omega=0\,,

(52)

up to a constant factor, expressing the notion that volumes remain invariant under parallel transport. The canonical invariant volume form on a Riemannian manifold, the
Riemannian volume form
is defined by

\differential V:=\sqrt{\det g}\;\differential x^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\differential x^{n}\,.

(53)

The Riemannian volume form’s defining property is its parallelity with respect to the LC-connection, i.e.
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-14.75882pt{\text{LC}}\kern 8.04631pt}}_{{\kern-29.28188pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.04631pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-14.75882pt{\text{LC}}\kern 8.04631pt}}_{{\kern-29.28188pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.04631pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-10.27547pt{\text{LC}}\kern 5.44908pt}}_{{\kern-20.64906pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.44908pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{LC}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-9.1088pt{\text{LC}}\kern 4.28241pt}}_{{\kern-19.48239pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.65282pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6014pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.71527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.28241pt}}}\,\differential V=0
.

4.3
Previous Studies on Geometric Priors

Historically, it was Jeffreys who first introduced a canonical,
“geometric”
prior
\pi_{\text{J}}(\theta)\sim\sqrt{\det\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{F}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}g^{{\kern-7.46219pt{\text{F}}\kern 4.15245pt}}_{{\kern-14.49915pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.15245pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{F}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}g^{{\kern-7.46219pt{\text{F}}\kern 4.15245pt}}_{{\kern-14.49915pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.15245pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{F}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}g^{{\kern-4.90878pt{\text{F}}\kern 2.51294pt}}_{{\kern-9.93518pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.51294pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{F}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}g^{{\kern-4.1908pt{\text{F}}\kern 1.79495pt}}_{{\kern-9.2172pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.79495pt}}}(\theta)}
, the so-called Jeffreys prior

[
15
]
. From a statistics standpoint, Jeffreys prior is the correct uniform prior distribution for an inference problem defined by the Fisher metric, due to its invariance under reparameterizations of the statistical model. From a geometric perspective,
\pi_{J}
is the unique Riemannian covolume (
\differential V_{J}=\pi_{J}\;\differential x^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\differential x^{n}
) on the potentially-curved statistical manifold. Thereby, Jeffreys volume form
\differential V_{J}
, which is parallel to the LC-connection, correctly defines integration over functions on the statistical manifold
\mathcal{M}

[
15
,
8
]
.

Takeuchi and Amari’s highly innovative work on geometric priors
[
4
]
generalizes Jeffreys prior to a parametrized prior family induced by the dual, parametrized
\alpha
-connections
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\alpha,\alpha^{*})}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[23.22719pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[23.22719pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[13.69128pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.42235pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-21.84871pt{(\alpha,\alpha^{*})}\kern 8.04631pt}}_{{\kern-54.28279pt\mathchoice{\makebox[23.22719pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[23.22719pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[13.69128pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.42235pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.44908pt}}_{{\kern-39.07832pt\mathchoice{\makebox[23.22719pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[23.22719pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[13.69128pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[10.42235pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.28241pt}}}
. They define the
\alpha
-priors as the covolumes of the
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\alpha)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.94563pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.96118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\omega^{{\kern-12.09451pt{(\alpha)}\kern (56)

In the limit of
\alpha\to 1
, the
\alpha
-divergence recovers the Kullback-Leibler divergence. Accordingly, the limit of
\alpha\to 1
gives the covolumes of the KL-divergence. This result is quickly confirmed by directly deriving the KL-covolumes for its induced geometry. As expected, the covolumes for the KL-divergence-induced connections

4.4
The Rényi-Priors and Hartigan’s Prior Family

This Section derives the volume forms that are parallel to the dual Rényi-induced connections given in Section
2.5
, as well as the corresponding covolumes which we call the
Rényi-priors
. The technical computation of these volume forms is based on the discussion of Takeuchi and Amari’s
\alpha
-priors (see
Equation 54
) presented in Calin and Udriste
[
25
]
. For a detailed derivation, the reader is referred to
A
. The link of these Rényi-priors to a prior family proposed by Hartigan
[
28
]
is elucidated.

To begin with, consider the Rényi-volume-forms,

From these results, several interesting findings emerge:

1.

It is evident that the Rényi-priors coincide with Jeffreys prior for
\rho=\frac{1}{2}
. Also, the limit of
\rho\to\frac{1}{2}
in
Equation 68
(and
Equation 69
) is distinct in that this is the only choice for which the volume forms are symmetric

(70)

Of course, this is in accordance with expectations, as
\rho=\frac{1}{2}
makes the Rényi divergence coincide with the
symmetric
Bhattacharyya divergence
D_{\text{B}}
, see Section
2.5
. It is straightforward to show that the Jeffreys prior
\pi_{J}
is the canonical uniform prior in the (self-dual) Bhattacharyya-connection’s affine coordinates.

2.

The Rényi-priors recover the KL-divergence-induced volume forms for
\rho=1
(which are also reached for
\alpha=-1
), reproducing the
\rho\to 1
(
\alpha\to-1
) limit at the level of divergences
[
6
]
. In fact, comparison of
Equation 68
in the limit of
\rho\to 1
with
Equation 56
allows for a consistency check
[
4
,
25
]

.

4.

The fact that the
\rho
-covolumes in
Equation 68
and
Equation 69
differ from the
\alpha
-covolumes (
Equation 54
) by a factor of
\rho^{n/2}
is expected, since conformal changes of the metric cause a scaling with the
(n/2)
-th power of the conformal factor in the volume form. This is however insignificant as volume forms are only defined up to a constant factor.

5.

Comparison of the Rényi-priors with
Equation 54
shows that, up to a power of the conformal factor, the dual volume forms induced by the Rényi divergence can be translated to those derived from
\alpha
-divergences (
Equation 54
) by the parameter choice
\rho=\frac{(1-\alpha)}{2}
. For the covolumes in
Equation 68
, one finds for instance

(72)

This result is non-trivial in light of the fact that the Rényi divergence’s logarithmic structure does not emerge from the
\alpha
-divergence by merely choosing this alternative parametrization. Furthermore, Sections
2
and
3.3
clarifies that the geometries are not the same.

6.

Crucially, Amari and Takeuchi
[
4
]
recognize the reparameterization the
\alpha
-priors,
\alpha^{\prime}=\frac{(1-\alpha)}{2}
, as a special choice for which certain asymptotic equivalences of statistical estimators are reached. The projected Bayes estimator asymptotically coincides with the bias-corrected maximum likelihood estimator in this reparameterization of the
\alpha
-priors
[
4
]
. Technically, this reparameterization is an artifact of
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\alpha)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.94563pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.96118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Gamma^{{\kern-12.0012pt{(\alpha)}\kern 4.94446pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.69165pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.77974pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-27.2816pt\mathchoice{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.94563pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.96118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.94446pt{ijk}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\alpha)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.94563pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.96118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Gamma^{{\kern-12.0012pt{(\alpha)}\kern 4.94446pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.69165pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.77974pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-27.2816pt\mathchoice{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.94563pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.96118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.94446pt{ijk}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\alpha)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.94563pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.96118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Gamma^{{\kern-8.1348pt{(\alpha)}\kern 3.0625pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.69165pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.77974pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-19.04938pt\mathchoice{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.94563pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.96118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.0625pt{ijk}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\alpha)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.94563pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.96118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Gamma^{{\kern-7.2598pt{(\alpha)}\kern 2.1875pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.69165pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.77974pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-18.17438pt\mathchoice{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.94563pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.96118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.1875pt{ijk}}}}
’s scaling with the Amari-Chentsov tensor
C_{ijk}
in
Equation 23
. Moreover, Takeuchi and Amari
[
4
]
state that the
\alpha
-priors coincide with a parametrized prior family introduced by Hartigan for this choice
[
28
,
4
]
, thus giving geometric validation to Hartigan’s statistics result. However, this is presented as a phenomenological result, without an a priori geometric reason as to why
\rho=\frac{(1-\alpha)}{2}

should
be of particular significance
[
4
]
.

We thus come to our main result.
Equation 71
and
Equation 72
show that the Rényi-priors offer a geometric rationale: the choice of
\rho=\frac{(1-\alpha)}{2}
gives the covolumes of the Rényi-geometry for
\mathcal{P}_{e}
(
\mathcal{P}_{m}
). To put it differently, Hartigan’s prior family coincides with the family of priors induced by the Rényi-geometry. The remainder of this Section is reserved for a derivation of this result.

In his 1964 and 1965
[
37
,
38
]
works on asymptotically unbiased prior distributions in statistics, Hartigan introduced a parametrized family of priors
\{\pi_{\alpha_{H}}\}
. Notably, Hartigan
[
37
,
38
]
motivates this prior family solely from a statistics point of view, not a geometric one. In his later work
[
28
]
, Hartigan gives the following definition for this parametrized family of invariant priors,

\partial_{i}\log\pi_{\alpha_{H}}:=(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{F}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}g^{{\kern-7.46219pt{\text{F}}\kern 4.15245pt{-1}}}_{{\kern-14.49915pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.15245pt\mathchoice{\makebox[8.50157pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.50157pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.08333pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.91666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{F}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}g^{{\kern-7.46219pt{\text{F}}\kern 4.15245pt{-1}}}_{{\kern-14.49915pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.15245pt\mathchoice{\makebox[8.50157pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.50157pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.08333pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.91666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{F}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}g^{{\kern-4.90878pt{\text{F}}\kern 2.51294pt{-1}}}_{{\kern-9.93518pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.51294pt\mathchoice{\makebox[8.50157pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.50157pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.08333pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.91666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{F}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}g^{{\kern-4.1908pt{\text{F}}\kern 1.79495pt{-1}}}_{{\kern-9.2172pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.79495pt\mathchoice{\makebox[8.50157pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.50157pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.08333pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.91666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}})^{jk}\;\mathbb{P}_{\theta}\left[\alpha_{H}\cdot\partial_{i}\log p_{\theta}\;\partial_{j}\log p_{\theta}\;\partial_{k}\log p_{\theta}+\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\log p_{\theta}\;\partial_{k}\log p_{\theta}\right]\,.

(73)

We denote Hartigan’s parameter by
\alpha_{H}
to distinguish the
\alpha_{H}
-priors from Takeuchi and Amari’s
\alpha
-priors. Moreover, we assume Hartigan’s notation
\mathbb{P}_{\theta}[f]
for the conditional expected value of a function over data space, i.e.
\mathbb{P}_{\theta}[f]:=\smallint\differential\mu(y)\;\mathcal{L}(y|\theta)\,f(y)
. The following derivation of this result is not limited to
\mathcal{P}_{e}
or
\mathcal{P}_{m}
, but holds true for statistical manifolds constructed from any suitable family of probability distributions.

Evaluation the parallelity condition,
0=(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\rho)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.39902pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.34447pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.53177pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-14.50189pt{(\rho)}\kern (75)

Comparison of
Equation 75
and
Equation 73
immediately shows that
\alpha_{H}=\rho
, i.e. Hartigan’s prior family with its original parametrization, is precisely the prior family of the Rényi-geometry. This result sustains the findings of Takeuchi and Amari
[
4
]
whilst offering a new explanation as to why the reparameterization
\alpha^{\prime}=\frac{(1-\alpha)}{2}
holds particular geometric and statistical meaning: this choice recovers Rényi’s uniform priors.

5
Conclusions and Outlook

Building on the results by Eguchi
[
17
]
, Jeffreys
[
15
]
, as well as Takeuchi and Amari
[
4
,
1
]
, this work explored the information geometry induced by the statistical Rényi divergence. Particular emphasis was placed on the role of certain geometric quantities, such as dual covolumes, in the context of statistical inference. Our main conclusions and findings can be stated as follows:

1.

Both, the metric tensor as well as the dual connections were computed from the Rényi-divergence. The Rényi-geometry’s metric tensor was found to differ from the Fisher information metric by a conformal factor
\rho
. In spite of the fact that the dual Rényi-connections are not symmetric in their parameters in the sense of
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\rho^{*})}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.34901pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.34901pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.59447pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.78177pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-16.75189pt{(\rho^{*})}\kern 8.04631pt}}_{{\kern-36.3397pt\mathchoice{\makebox[13.34901pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.34901pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.59447pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.78177pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.04631pt}}_{{\kern-35.86412pt\mathchoice{\makebox[14.91446pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.91446pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.9778pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.69843pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.04631pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(-\rho)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.91446pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.91446pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.9778pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.69843pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-11.25862pt{(-\rho)}\kern 5.44908pt}}_{{\kern-25.3507pt\mathchoice{\makebox[14.91446pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.91446pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.9778pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.69843pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.44908pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(-\rho)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.91446pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.91446pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.9778pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.69843pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\nabla^{{\kern-10.09195pt{(-\rho)}\kern 4.28241pt}}_{{\kern-24.18402pt\mathchoice{\makebox[14.91446pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.91446pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.9778pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.69843pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.28241pt}}}
, dual metric compatibility holds, as is requisite for an operable information geometry. In general, the dual Rényi-geometry is genuinely different from Amari’s
\alpha
-geometry, as indicated by the failure of any general coordinate transformation to make the parametrized
\rho
-scaling and
\alpha
-scaling, respectively, coincide. As required, these geometries coincide in the limit of
(\rho\to 1),\,(\alpha\to-1)
, respectively.

2.

Dual parametrized Laplace-Beltrami-operators were calculated from the Rényi-geometry’s dual connections. The structure of these operators exhibits a
\rho
-scaling which
cannot
be made to coincide with that of Amari’s
\alpha
-LB-operator by way of a reparameterization
\rho(\alpha)
, supporting the recognition that these information geometries are inherently different in structure.

3.

The dual Rényi-priors were derived as covolumes of the dual volume forms, which are parallel to the individual dual Rényi-connections. The results confirm the duality of the exponential and mixture family
[
16
]
at the level of volume forms, since

3.05002pt}}_{{\kern-23.86276pt\mathchoice{\makebox[13.34901pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.34901pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.59447pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.78177pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.05002pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\rho^{*})}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.34901pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.34901pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.59447pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.78177pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\omega^{{\kern-9.07147pt{(\rho^{*})}\kern 2.17859pt}}_{{\kern-22.99133pt\mathchoice{\makebox[13.34901pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.34901pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.59447pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.78177pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.17859pt}}}_{m}
. As required, the Rényi-priors recover the self-dual Jeffreys prior for
\rho\to(1/2)
and coincide with Amari and Takeuchi’s
(\alpha=1)
-priors for
(\rho=1)
. Up to a power of the conformal factor, the reparameterization
\rho(\alpha)=(1+\alpha)/2
translates the canonical Rényi-priors to the
\alpha
-priors. Importantly, Amari and Takeuchi note that the choice
\alpha\to(1+\alpha)/2
is distinct, as it leads to certain unique asymptotic behaviours of statistical estimators, all while also recovering Hartigan’s prior family
[
4
,
28
]
. We find that for this choice, the
\alpha
-priors become the Rényi-priors. To put it differently, Hartigan’s prior family with its original
\alpha_{H}
-parametrization precisely coincides with the Rényi-priors (i.e.
\rho=\alpha_{H}
).

The following table presents the essential geometric quantities derived for the Rényi-geometry in this work, in direct comparison with those of some well-established information geometries, namely the Bhattacharyya-geometry, the KL-geometry, as well as Amari’s
\alpha
-geometry.

Overview of the Bhattacharyya-geometry alongside the KL-geometry.

-Divergence

Rényi
\rho
-Divergence

Divergence

D_{\alpha}[\theta:\theta^{\prime}]=\frac{4}{1-\alpha^{2}}\left(1-\int\differential\mu(y)p^{\frac{1-\alpha}{2}}(p^{\prime})^{\frac{1+\alpha}{2}}\right)

3.05002pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\rho),(\rho^{*})}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[27.37767pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[27.37767pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.30005pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.28575pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\omega^{{\kern-14.57545pt{(\rho),(\rho^{*})}\kern 2.17859pt}}_{{\kern-41.77072pt\mathchoice{\makebox[27.37767pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[27.37767pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[16.30005pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[12.28575pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.17859pt}}}\,.

(76)

Furthermore, future research is needed to determine how the extremality of the family of Gaussian probability distributions arises in the context of volume integration on the statistical manifold. In this effort, we plan to examine the link between isoprobability surfaces and enclosed probability mass for the geometry of different statistical families. As hinted at in Section
3
, more work is needed to examine the dual RLB-operator’s effects in judging the optimality of statistical estimators and predictive densities. In addition, we plan to explicate the link between RLB-operators and Rényi-covolumes. Further topics of interest for the Rényi-geometry include Lie derivatives and Killing fields for quantifying statistical degeneracies as isometries, particularly since the conventional definition of the Lie derivative relies on metric compatibility of the covariant derivative.

Appendix A

4
, the following technical derivation is based on the derivation of Takeuchi and Amari’s

-priors (see
Equation 54
) presented in Calin and Udriste

.

and thus

\displaystyle=(\det\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{F}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}g^{{\kern-7.46219pt{\text{F}}\kern 4.15245pt}}_{{\kern-14.49915pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.15245pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{F}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}g^{{\kern-7.46219pt{\text{F}}\kern 4.15245pt}}_{{\kern-14.49915pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.15245pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{F}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}g^{{\kern-4.90878pt{\text{F}}\kern 2.51294pt}}_{{\kern-9.93518pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.51294pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\text{F}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}g^{{\kern-4.1908pt{\text{F}}\kern 1.79495pt}}_{{\kern-9.2172pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.15974pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.19862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.28473pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.79495pt}}})^{-\left(\rho-\frac{1}{2}\right)}\;\differential V_{\rho}
